description: "How the ZX Spectrum Next takes sound from its Raspberry Pi: the I2S link on four wires and who drives its clock, NextReg $A2's stereo, mono, mute and EAR settings, a signed sample turned into a 10-bit word, when each word is latched, the Pi's share of the mixer, reading samples through NextRegs $2C-$2E, the EAR rule that lets the Next load a tape from the Pi, the Next's own sound sent back, and how Klive stands in for the Pi."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# Audio from the Raspberry Pi: Samples on Four Wires

Open a Spectrum Next, and next to the FPGA you find a whole second computer: a Raspberry Pi Zero, with
an ARM processor, hundreds of megabytes of memory and a Linux of its own. Compared with the Z80, it is a
lorry parked next to a bicycle. A program on it can decode an MP3, speak from text, or pretend to be any
sound chip you like. But a computer that clever is no use to the Next's sound unless its sound can get
in. How do you pour the output of a Linux machine into a 1980s home computer, and even trick that home
computer into loading a tape from it?

The answer matters as soon as a program wants more than the Next can make by itself. Music recorded in
full, a long piece of speech, a whole tape played as sound for the Next to load: these are jobs for a
machine with memory to spare and a processor to decode. The Pi does the heavy lifting, and the Next only
has to listen.

Read [The Big Picture: Four Sources, One Mixer](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/)
first: it introduces the Pi as one of the four kinds of source. The chapter on
[the beeper, EAR and MIC](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/beeper/) explains port `$FE`
bit 6 and the MIC line, which the Pi's tape trick plugs into, and the previous chapter,
[The 8-Bit DACs: Four Channels, Six Sound Cards](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/dacs/),
shows NextRegs `$2C` to `$2E` from the writing side. Samples and digital sound are explained in the
background chapter [How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/).

## The road ahead

First, the link itself: four wires, a clock the Pi drives, and words that take turns, left and right. Then
the one register that controls it all, and what it does to the two sides: stereo, mono from either word,
or a side muted. We look at how a sample from the Pi becomes the Next's 10-bit word, and at the exact moment
each word arrives. From there the words go three ways: into the mixer, to three NextRegs a program can read,
and, the trick I would ask you to remember, through a threshold into the tape input, so that the Next can
load a program the Pi plays as sound. That threshold has a surprise in it, by the way. We end with the way
back, the Next's own sound sent to the Pi, and with how Klive stands in for a Pi it does not emulate.

<Callout type="info">
The numbers are for the Next's **28 MHz master clock**. The Pi's samples are **signed 16-bit numbers**,
−32,768 to 32,767, and the Next's are **10-bit words**, `$000` to `$3FF`. The captures come from Klive's
emulated Next at 48,000 samples a second, from programs checked to do what the text says. **Klive does not
emulate the Pi**: a sample source takes its place, a WAV file or a tone, and feeds real samples into the
Next's receiving half of the link, so what you see is the Next's half working as it does on the hardware.
</Callout>

## Four wires and a clock from the Pi

The Pi and the Next talk sound over **I2S**, a link made for exactly one job: carrying a stream of stereo
samples, one bit at a time. It needs only a few wires:

<figure>
{/* fig:link */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 360" role="img" aria-label="A diagram of the link between the Raspberry Pi and the Next. On the left, the Pi, which drives the bit clock and the word select into the Next, and sends its samples on one data pin. The Next's receiver latches a left and a right word. NextReg $A2 chooses and mutes them, and they go to the NextRegs $2C, $2D and $2E for a program to read and into the mixer. Above, routed to EAR, the receiver's words pass a threshold into port $FE bit 6 and the MIC line. Below, the mixer's sum goes back to the Pi on the other data pin. NextReg $A2 bit 4 decides which data pin is which." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="350" y="264.3" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e5484d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">both sides</text>
  <path d="M280,250 L134,250" fill="none" stroke="#e5484d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#pi-fl-2)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <text x="160" y="243" fill="#e5484d" fontSize="13.5">the Next's mix</text>
  <text x="0" y="322" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">data pins: NextReg $A2 bit 4 decides which pin is which</text>
  <text x="0" y="346" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">the Pi drives both clocks; the Next only listens to them</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:link */}
<figcaption>The link between the Pi and the Next. The Pi drives both clocks; one data pin carries the Pi's samples in, the other the Next's mix out, and NextReg <code>$A2</code> bit 4 decides which pin is which.</figcaption>
</figure>

- The **bit clock** ticks once for every bit sent.
- The **word select** says whose word the bits belong to: low for the left word, high for the right one.
- **Two data pins** carry the bits themselves, one in each direction.

On the Next, **the Pi is always the master**: it drives the bit clock and the word select, and the Next only
listens to them. So the Pi, not the Next, decides the sample rate. Whatever rate the Pi's software chooses,
44,100 or 48,000 a second or anything else, the Next takes the words as they come. Well, that is one fewer
thing for you to set up.

Here is one sample period on the wires:

{/* fig:timing */}

The data line carries each word's bits, most significant first, starting one clock after the word select changes. A mark at the word select's rise, in the middle of the period, says the left word is latched there; a mark at its fall, at the end of the period, says the right word is latched there." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>One sample period, drawn with 16-bit words. The word select is low for the left word and high for the right; each word starts one clock after the change, most significant bit first.</figcaption>
</figure>

Each word starts one clock after the word select changes, its most significant bit first. And notice where the
words are **latched**, that is, where the Next takes a finished word and starts using it: the left word at the
word select's rise, in the middle of the period, and the right word at its fall, at the end. We will come back
to that half a period of difference in a moment.

## One register: NextReg `$A2`

Everything the Next does with the link is set in one register, the **PI I2S Audio Control register, NextReg
`$A2`**:

<figure>
{/* fig:a2 */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 200" role="img" aria-label="NextReg $A2 as a strip of eight bits, bit 7 on the left. Bits 7 and 6 enable the left and right words: 00 off, 01 mono from the right word, 10 mono from the left word, 11 stereo. Bit 5 must be 0. Bit 4 sets which data pin is which. Bits 3 and 2 mute the left and the right side. Bit 1 must be 1. Bit 0 sends the Pi's audio to EAR, port $FE bit 6." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="128.1" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">7</text>
  <text x="204.4" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">6</text>
  <text x="280.6" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">5</text>
  <text x="356.9" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">4</text>
  <text x="433.1" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">3</text>
  <text x="509.4" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">2</text>
  <text x="585.6" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">1</text>
  <text x="661.9" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">0</text>
  <text x="0" y="50" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">NextReg</text>
  <text x="0" y="68" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">$A2</text>
  <rect x="92" y="30" width="148.5" height="56" rx="5" fill="#a855f7" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="166.3" y="54" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">enable:</text>
  <text x="166.3" y="72" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">left, right</text>
  <rect x="244.5" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.4" />
  <text x="280.6" y="64" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">0</text>
  <rect x="320.8" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="#a855f7" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="356.9" y="54" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">data</text>
  <text x="356.9" y="72" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">pins</text>
  <rect x="397" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="#a855f7" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="433.1" y="54" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">mute</text>
  <text x="433.1" y="72" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">left</text>
  <rect x="473.3" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="#a855f7" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="509.4" y="54" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">mute</text>
  <text x="509.4" y="72" textAnchor="middle" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">right</text>
  <rect x="549.5" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.4" />
  <text x="585.6" y="64" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">1</text>
  <rect x="625.8" y="30" width="72.3" height="56" rx="5" fill="#3b82f6" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#3b82f6" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="661.9" y="54" textAnchor="middle" fill="#3b82f6" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">to</text>
  <text x="661.9" y="72" textAnchor="middle" fill="#3b82f6" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">EAR</text>
  <text x="90" y="120" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">bits 7–6: 00 off, 01 mono from the right word, 10 mono from the left word, 11 stereo</text>
  <text x="90" y="142" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">bit 0: the Pi's audio leaves the mixer and drives port $FE bit 6 and the MIC line instead</text>
  <text x="90" y="184" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">after a reset: $02, off</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:a2 */}
<figcaption>NextReg <code>$A2</code>. Bits 5 and 1 are fixed: write bit 5 as 0 and bit 1 as 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **Bits 7 and 6** enable the left and the right word. Both clear means the link is off: the Next ignores the
  Pi, and the receiver is held at rest. That is how it starts after a reset.
- **Bit 4** chooses which of the two data pins brings the Pi's samples in and which takes the Next's mix out.
  The register list calls the two settings "hats" (`0`) and "pi" (`1`).
- **Bits 3 and 2** mute the left and the right side.
- **Bit 0** sends the Pi's sound to EAR, the tape input, instead of to the mixer. More on that below.

Bits 7 and 6 do one more thing. With only one of them set, the side whose bit is clear does not go silent; it
takes the other side's word. So `10` plays the Pi's left word on both sides, and `01` plays the right word on
both. Here is a stream with a 1,000 Hz sine on its left word and a 1,500 Hz sine on its right, heard four ways:

<figure>
{/* fig:modes */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 724" role="img" aria-label="Eight captures of five milliseconds, in four pairs, the left and the right side, from $000 to $3FF. The Pi streams a 1,000 Hz sine on its left word and a 1,500 Hz sine on its right word. Stereo, NextReg $A2 = $C0: 1,000 Hz on the left, 1,500 Hz on the right. Mono from the left word, $80: 1,000 Hz on both sides. Mono from the right word, $40: 1,500 Hz on both sides. Stereo with the left side muted, $C8: the left side flat at $200, 1,500 Hz on the right." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The same stream in four settings of NextReg <code>$A2</code>, each side as the mixer gets it. A muted side holds the rest word <code>$200</code>.</figcaption>
</figure>

A muted side, a link that is off and a stream routed to EAR all give the mixer the same word: `$200`, the
middle of the range, which is silence.

## From a sample to a word

The Pi's software sends ordinary signed samples, typically 16 bits each, sometimes 24 or 32. The Next keeps
only what it needs:

<figure>
{/* fig:word */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 300" role="img" aria-label="Three strips of bits. Top, a signed 16-bit sample from the Pi, bit 15, the sign, on the left. The receiver keeps the top 13 bits, bits 15 to 3. The Next uses the top 10, bits 15 to 6, and inverts the sign bit, which turns the signed number into one counted from 0: the bottom strip, a 10-bit word. Below, three examples: the most negative sample, -32,768, becomes $000; silence, 0, becomes $200; the most positive, 32,767, becomes $3FF." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="0" y="246" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontFamily="monospace">0 → $200</text>
  <text x="0" y="268" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontFamily="monospace">32,767 → $3FF</text>
  <text x="330" y="224" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">10-bit word = ((sample &gt;&gt; 6) &amp; $3FF) xor $200</text>
  <text x="330" y="246" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">a 16-, 24- or 32-bit slot gives the same word</text>

{/* /fig:word */}
<figcaption>A signed sample from the Pi becomes the Next's 10-bit word: the top ten bits, with the sign bit inverted.</figcaption>
</figure>

The receiver keeps the **top 13 bits** of each word as it arrives, whatever the word's length. That is why a
16-, a 24- and a 32-bit stream all work: the extra bits fall off the bottom. The Next then uses the **top 10**
of those and inverts the sign bit. Inverting the sign bit is the cheapest trick in the book for turning a signed
number into one that counts from 0, the form every other source of the mixer uses: −32,768 becomes `$000`,
0 becomes `$200` and 32,767 becomes `$3FF`. If you like formulas, the word is
`((sample >> 6) & $3FF) xor $200` for a 16-bit sample.

Ten bits sounds stingy next to the Pi's sixteen, doesn't it? But it matches the rest of the Next: a DAC channel
has 8 bits, and the mixer's whole range for a side is 13. The Pi gets more steps than any one DAC.

## When a word arrives

The left and the right word of one sample do not arrive together. Remember the latches: the left word at the
middle of a sample period, the right word at its end. Here is a slow stream, 1,000 samples a second, whose
samples alternate between two levels, the same on both sides:

<figure>

<figcaption>A stream of 1,000 samples a second. Each side steps once a millisecond, but the left side steps half a millisecond before the right.</figcaption>
</figure>

At a real sample rate the gap is tiny, about 10 µs at 48,000 samples a second, and no ear can hear it. But a
program that reads both words and compares them can see it, and so, as you will see, can the tape input. Two
more details: a link switched on starts at the rest word and takes the Pi's words from the next latch, and a
link switched off goes back to rest at once. If the Pi stops clocking, nothing is latched any more, and the
receiver simply holds its last two words.

## Into the mixer

Each side's word goes into the mixer as it is: **0 to 1,023 units**, one unit a step.

<figure>
{/* fig:budget */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 170" role="img" aria-label="A bar drawn to scale on the mixer's range of 0 to 8,191 for one side. The Pi's word takes 0 to 1,023 units, an eighth of the range. At rest, the word $200, it adds 512, the middle of its range." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="0" y="22" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">the Pi's word on one side: 0–1,023 units, one unit a step</text>
  <rect x="0" y="32" width="698" height="30" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.5" strokeOpacity="0.3" />
  <rect x="0" y="32" width="87.2" height="30" fill="#a855f7" fillOpacity="0.7" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="1" />
  <line x1="43.6" y1="32" x2="43.6" y2="90" stroke="#a855f7" strokeWidth="2" strokeDasharray="5 4" />
  <text x="51.6" y="88" fill="#a855f7" fontSize="13.5">at rest: $200, 512 units</text>
  <line x1="0" y1="122" x2="700" y2="122" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <line x1="0" y1="117" x2="0" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="0" y="148" textAnchor="start" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">0</text>
  <line x1="43.6" y1="117" x2="43.6" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="43.6" y="148" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">512</text>
  <line x1="87.2" y1="117" x2="87.2" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="87.2" y="148" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">1,023</text>
  <line x1="173.8" y1="117" x2="173.8" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="173.8" y="148" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">2,040</text>
  <line x1="349" y1="117" x2="349" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="349" y="148" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">4,095</text>
  <line x1="698" y1="117" x2="698" y2="127" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="698" y="148" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">8,191</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:budget */}
<figcaption>The Pi's share of one side of the mixer, to scale: 0 to 1,023 units, 512 at rest.</figcaption>
</figure>

One unit a step is a quarter of a DAC step, which the mixer counts four times. So a Pi stream at full scale
swings 1,023 units, about the same as one DAC channel at full scale, and twice the beeper's 512. At rest it
adds 512 units, the other part of the 1,536 a silent Next sits at, next to the DACs' 1,024. The mixer chapter's
[Headroom](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#headroom) shows how much room is left for
the rest.

## Reading the Pi's samples

A program can also read the words, through the same three NextRegs that write the DACs. NextReg `$2C` reads the
left word and NextReg `$2E` the right one, but a NextReg has only eight bits, and a word has ten. So the read
comes in two parts:

<figure>
{/* fig:read */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 276" role="img" aria-label="A diagram of reading the Pi's left word. The 10-bit word is drawn as ten cells.   <text x="140" y="236" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">then a read of NextReg $2D</text>
  <text x="140" y="254.5" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">returns the low two bits</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:read */}
<figcaption>Reading a 10-bit word through 8-bit NextRegs: NextReg <code>$2C</code> returns the top eight bits and keeps the bottom two for NextReg <code>$2D</code>.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. A read of NextReg `$2C` returns bits 9–2 of the left word, as the selection gives it: muted or off, that is
   the rest word. At the same moment it copies bits 1–0 into a latch.
2. A read of NextReg `$2D` returns those two latched bits in its bits 7 and 6.

NextReg `$2E` works the same way for the right word, through the same latch. Why the latch? Because the word can
change between your two reads, and the latch makes sure the low bits belong to the same word as the high bits
you just read. Most programs need only the top eight bits anyway: `$2C` alone gives you a perfectly good 8-bit
sample. With nothing connected, `$2C` and `$2E` read `$80` and `$2D` reads `$00`, the rest word in two parts.

## Loading a tape from the Pi

Here is my favourite trick of the whole link. Set bit 0 of NextReg `$A2`, and the Pi's words stop going to the
mixer. Instead they drive a single bit, the **EAR bit**, which goes where a tape recorder's signal goes: into
**port `$FE` bit 6**, the bit a tape loader reads. A program on the Pi plays a
tape as sound, and the Next loads it, none the wiser. Hey, it even sounds like a tape loading, because the EAR
bit also goes into the MIC line, which the beeper chapter showed you reaches the speaker.

How does a 10-bit word become one bit? Not with a plain threshold at the middle. A tape signal is never clean:
it has noise on it, and at the moment a slow wave crosses a threshold, the noise would flip the bit back and
forth several times, and every flip looks like an edge to a loader. So the Next uses **hysteresis**, two
thresholds instead of one:

- The bit **rises** when the top two bits of the left word and of the right word, ORed together, read `11`.
- It **falls** when they read `00`: both words' top two bits are `00`, so both are below `$100`, a quarter of
  the range.
- Anything in between, and the bit **holds** what it had.

Now the surprise I promised. You might expect the bit to rise only at three quarters of the range, at `$300`,
where a word's own top bits read `11`. But the rule ORs the bits of *two* words, and remember, the left word is
latched half a period before the right. On a rising wave, at the moment it crosses the middle, the left word has
just stepped to `$200` or a little above, top bits `10`, while the right word is still just below the middle,
top bits `01`. And `10` OR `01` is `11`. So the bit rises right at the **upward zero crossing**, and falls only
when the wave has dropped below a quarter. Here it is on a slow, noisy 250 Hz wave:

<figure>
{/* fig:ear */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 400" role="img" aria-label="Three rows over 12 milliseconds. Top, the Pi's stream: a slow 250 Hz sine with noise on it, from -32,768 to 32,767, with a dashed line at -16,384, where both words' top two bits read 00, and a line at 0, where a rising wave first makes them read 11 together. Middle, the EAR bit captured from the machine: a clean square wave that rises as the wave crosses 0 upwards and falls once it has dropped below -16,384, two edges a cycle. Bottom, for comparison, what a plain threshold at 0 would give on the same wave: a square wave that chatters, switching many times at every crossing." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:ear */}
<figcaption>The EAR bit on a noisy 250 Hz wave from the Pi, routed with NextReg <code>$A2</code> = <code>$C1</code>. The captured bit rises at the upward zero crossing and falls below −16,384: two clean edges a cycle. A plain threshold at 0, worked out for the same wave, chatters at every crossing.</figcaption>
</figure>

Two clean edges a cycle, while a plain threshold chatters. For a loader, that is the difference between loading
and "R Tape loading error". And for a real tape signal, which swings hard from one side to the other, it does not
matter where exactly between the middle and a quarter the bit flips: the edges come at the same rate, and that
is all a loader counts. A Z80 loop that reads port `$FE` bit 6 while the Pi streams a 1,000 Hz square wave
counts 2,000 edges a second, as it would from a tape.

Port `$FE` bit 6 is the EAR bit XORed with the real tape input, ORed with the others the beeper chapter lists, so a
quiet tape input leaves it as it is. The EAR bit is 0 whenever the link is off or the routing bit is clear.

## The way back: the Next's sound to the Pi

The link carries sound the other way too. On the other data pin, the Next sends the Pi its **whole mixed sound**,
both sides, the same 13-bit sums the mixer adds, with the top bit inverted to make them signed again, at the
top of each word. The Pi's software can record it, analyse it, or send it on, to a network or a Bluetooth speaker,
say. The Next needs no setting for this beyond enabling the link: whenever the Pi clocks a word in, the Next
clocks its own word out.

## How Klive does it

Klive emulates **the Next's half of the link, and nothing of the Pi**. A **sample source** stands where the Pi
would be: a WAV file or a tone, chosen with the [`pi-audio` command](/en/reference/commands/#pi-audio). It hands
the core a block of samples before each frame, and the core's receiver latches them at the hardware's moments,
the left word in the middle of each sample period and the right word at its end, counted in 28 MHz clocks. From
there everything is the Next's own: the selection, muting and mono of NextReg `$A2`, the conversion to a 10-bit
word, the `$2C`/`$2D`/`$2E` reads with their latch, the mixer input, and the EAR bit with exactly the hardware's
rule, two-word OR included, into port `$FE` bit 6 and the MIC line.

The receiver catches up only when something looks: a NextReg read, a port `$FE` read, the mixer taking a sample.
The samples a frame needs are counted from the frame's start, so the fast frame and the debugger's
instruction-by-instruction run see exactly the same stream, and reverse debugging replays the samples it
recorded rather than asking the source again. Klive leaves out three things on purpose: the Pi itself (its
software, and the NextPi commands it would answer on its serial port), the Next's mix sent back to the Pi, and the
half-finished word a real receiver may latch in the instant you switch the link on. If you want to read the code
yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The receiver: latching, NextReg `$A2`'s selection, the `$2C`–`$2E` reads, the EAR bit | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-pi-i2s.c` |
| The sample sources: silence, a tone, a WAV file | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/piI2s/` (`PiI2sSource.ts`, `wavDecoder.ts`) |
| The `pi-audio` command | `src/renderer/appIde/commands/PiAudioCommand.ts` |
| The Pi's word in the sum | `zxnext-audio-mixer.c` beside the receiver |
| The EAR bit in the MIC line and port `$FE` | `zxnext-beeper.c` and `zxnext-ula.c` beside the receiver |

## What to take home

The Raspberry Pi inside the Next sends its sound over **I2S**: a bit clock and a word select, both driven by the
Pi, and a data pin each way. The Pi chooses the rate; the Next keeps the top 13 bits of each word, uses the top 10
with the sign bit inverted, and so turns −32,768, 0 and 32,767 into `$000`, `$200` and `$3FF`. The left word is
latched in the middle of each sample period, the right word at its end.

**NextReg `$A2`** controls it all: bits 7 and 6 enable the left and right word, and a side whose bit is clear takes
the other side's word; bits 3 and 2 mute; bit 4 swaps the data pins; bit 0 sends the sound to EAR. In the mixer
the Pi adds 0 to 1,023 units a side, 512 at rest. NextRegs `$2C` and `$2E` read the top eight bits of each word
and keep the bottom two for NextReg `$2D`.

Routed to EAR, the words drive port `$FE` bit 6 and the MIC line through **hysteresis**: up when the two words'
top two bits OR to `11`, which a rising wave reaches already at its zero crossing, down when both are below a
quarter. That is what lets the Next load a tape the Pi plays, without a single loader noticing. And on the other
pin, the Next's own mix goes back to the Pi. Klive plays the Pi's part with a sample source, and the Next's half
with the hardware's rules.20:I[8500,["545","$16","500","$17","436","$18","25","$19"],""]

2d:["$","figure",null,{"children":[["$","svg",null,{"viewBox":"0 0 700 200","role":"img","aria-label":"NextReg $A2 as a strip of eight bits, bit 7 on the left. Bits 7 and 6 enable the left and right words: 00 off, 01 mono from the right word, 10 mono from the left word, 11 stereo. Bit 5 must be 0. Bit 4 sets which data pin is which. 35:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The receiver keeps the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"top 13 bits"}]," of each word as it arrives, whatever the word’s length. That is why a\n16-, a 24- and a 32-bit stream all work: the extra bits fall off the bottom. The Next then uses the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"top 10"}],"\nof those and inverts the sign bit. Inverting the sign bit is the cheapest trick in the book for turning a signed\nnumber into one that counts from 0, the form every other source of the mixer uses: −32,768 becomes ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$000"}],",\n0 becomes ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$200"}]," and 32,767 becomes ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$3FF"}],". If you like formulas, the word is\n",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"((sample >> 6) & $3FF) xor $200"}]," for a 16-bit sample."]}]

3a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"At a real sample rate the gap is tiny, about 10 µs at 48,000 samples a second, and no ear can hear it. On a rising wave, at the moment it crosses the middle, the left word has\njust stepped to ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$200"}]," or a little above, top bits ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"10"}],", while the right word is still just below the middle,\ntop bits ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"01"}],". And ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"10"}]," OR ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"01"}]," is ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"11"}],". So the bit rises right at the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"upward zero crossing"}],", and falls only\nwhen the wave has dropped below a quarter. Here it is on a slow, noisy 250 Hz wave:"]}]
